========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I... I suppose I'm here because things just haven't been right lately. I mean, for a long time, actually. I've been in sales for years, you know, selling services, talking to people all day. You have to be upbeat for that, you have to have a certain energy. I used to be that guy. Ruben, the guy who could talk to anybody, strike up a conversation with a brick wall and walk away with a smile. But lately... well, most days I wake up and there's just this... this heavy, hollow feeling right here in my chest. It's like the moment I open my eyes, it's sitting there waiting for me. And it just doesn't go away. I try to push through it, I really do, but it's like that old Ruben is just gone. Completely vanished. I look in the mirror and I don't even recognize the guy staring back, because he's just so empty inside. It makes it nearly impossible to even get out of bed some mornings, let alone put on a suit and try to convince people to buy something. It's just this constant, heavy sadness that I can't shake, no matter what I do.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that's exactly it. It's actually funny you ask that, because my wife was just saying the exact same thing the other day, and... well, it's hard to admit, but she's right. I used to be a guy who always had something going on. I was on a Tuesday night bowling league for, gosh, maybe fifteen years? I'd look forward to it all week. The guys, the noise, the awful alley pizza... I loved every second of it. And on the weekends, you couldn't drag me out of the garden. I'd be out there on my hands and knees in the dirt, pruning the tomatoes, pulling weeds until the sun went down. It felt good, you know? Like I was really accomplishing something with my own two hands. 

And I always made sure we had a good time, my wife and I. We'd get dressed up on a Saturday. She absolutely loves those local art gallery shows, so I'd take her to those, walk around, look at the paintings, drink the cheap wine they hand out. Or we'd go dancing. We used to love going dancing together. 

But now? Now I... I don't know. I can't even fathom doing a single one of those things. Tuesday rolls around and the guys text me about bowling, and I just... I sit there in my armchair and I make up excuses. I tell them my knee is acting up, or I have extra paperwork. The garden is completely overgrown, it looks like an absolute jungle out there, but I look out the window and I just feel nothing. My wife asks about going to a gallery or maybe finding a place to dance, and I tell her I'm too tired, or my head hurts. I just sit there in the house. It’s like... none of it even feels worth the effort anymore. Why put on the shoes, why drive all the way down there, when I know I'm not going to feel a single ounce of joy? It's just too much work for absolutely zero payoff. The spark for all of it is just dead.

Well, let me think back... it’s been a while now. I’d say, if I really have to pin it down, it’s been about eight solid months of this. Eight months of waking up feeling like I'm made of lead. And I know exactly when it started, too, because it wasn't just out of the blue. 

You see, we had a bit of a corporate shakeup right around then. They brought in this new regional manager—this young guy, probably half my age, fresh out of some business school with all these fancy new spreadsheets and metrics for how we should be selling. Now, I’ve been doing this for decades, right? I know my clients. I know their kids' names, their golf handicaps, what kind of coffee they drink. But this new guy, he just started coming down on me, constantly. Nitpicking my call logs, questioning my methods, always breathing down my neck. 

And then... then came the real kicker. He started taking away my accounts. Accounts I had built from the absolute ground up, clients I had handled for years and years, just... stripped away and handed over to the junior guys who don't know the first thing about building a real relationship. I remember sitting in my car in a parking lot after he reassigned the Miller account—I'd had them for six years, doctor, six years!—and just feeling this... this crushing weight drop right onto my chest. That was about eight months ago. And ever since that week, ever since he started dismantling everything I worked so hard to build, that heavy, empty feeling just moved in and set up shop. It’s like he didn't just take my accounts, he took the whole wind out of my sails. And I just haven't been able to get it back since. Eight months of just... nothing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not like this. I mean, sure, I've had bad days. Everyone has bad days, right? You lose a big sale, you get a flat tire on the way to a meeting, you have a fight with your wife about whose turn it is to take out the recycling... you get down for a day or two. That's just life. That's just the cost of doing business, as they say. 

I remember back in, oh, it must have been '98 or '99, the market took a bit of a dive and things were tight. We had to skip our summer vacation that year, usually we'd go down to the Cape, rent a little place near the water... anyway, I was pretty bummed out about that. I was grumpy for a couple of weeks, sure. But I still went to work, I still bowled my Tuesday nights, I still cared about things. I bounced back. 

But this? This is entirely different. This isn't just being bummed out about a lost account or a missed vacation. This is... this is like someone flipped a switch inside me and just turned off the power completely. I've never, in my fifty-eight years on this earth, felt anything close to this. It's like I said, it's this physical weight, this absolute emptiness that just doesn't lift. I've always been the guy who could find the silver lining, the guy who could crack a joke and get everyone laughing even when the numbers were down. I prided myself on that. My resilience, you know? But now... I don't even know where that guy went. So, no, to answer your question, this is completely new territory for me. And it's terrifying, to be completely honest with you. It really is. I just don't know how to pull myself out of it because I've never been in a hole this deep before.

That was about eight months ago. And ever since that week, ever since he started dismantling everything I worked so hard to build, that heavy, empty feeling just moved in and set up shop. It’s like he didn't just take my accounts, he took the whole wind out of my sails. And I just haven't been able to get it back since. That's just the cost of doing business, as they say. \n\nI remember back in, oh, it must have been '98 or '99, the market took a bit of a dive and things were tight. I was grumpy for a couple of weeks, sure. But I still went to work, I still bowled my Tuesday nights, I still cared about things. I bounced back. \n\nBut this? This is entirely different. I've always been the guy who could find the silver lining, the guy who could crack a joke and get everyone laughing even when the numbers were down. I prided myself on that. My resilience, you know? Weight? Appetite? You know, I haven't really noticed any big changes there. I mean, my wife still makes dinner every night. We usually have chicken or a roast, maybe some potatoes... she's a wonderful cook, really she is. She always makes sure there's a vegetable on the plate, too, usually green beans or carrots. I still sit down at the table with her. I eat what she puts in front of me. I don't really taste it the way I used to, to be honest. It all kind of tastes like cardboard lately, just... fuel, I guess. But I still eat it. I haven't been skipping meals or anything. 

And as for my weight, I haven't stepped on a scale in a while, but my clothes still fit the same. My suits still button up just fine. I always wear a suit to work, you know, even on casual Fridays. I believe in looking professional. A good suit, a crisp shirt, a nice tie—it shows the client you respect them. Even with this new manager breathing down my neck and taking my accounts, I still make sure my tie is straight and my shoes are shined. I might feel like a ghost on the inside, but on the outside, I still look the part. So, no, my pants aren't falling off or anything. I'd say my weight has stayed pretty much the same. I haven't had to buy new belts or let anything out. It's just... I just eat because it's time to eat, not because I actually want to. But the amount? No, that hasn't changed.

It’s like... none of it even feels worth the effort anymore. Why put on the shoes, why drive all the way down there, when I know I'm not going to feel a single ounce of joy? It's just too much work for absolutely zero payoff. The spark for all of it is just dead.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the sadness and loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back... it’s been a while now. I’d say, if I really have to pin it down, it’s been about eight solid months of this. Eight months of waking up feeling like I'm made of lead. And I know exactly when it started, too, because it wasn't just out of the blue. \n\nYou see, we had a bit of a corporate shakeup right around then. They brought in this new regional manager—this young guy, probably half my age, fresh out of some business school with all these fancy new spreadsheets and metrics for how we should be selling. Now, I’ve been doing this for decades, right? I know my clients. I know their kids' names, their golf handicaps, what kind of coffee they drink. But this new guy, he just started coming down on me, constantly. Nitpicking my call logs, questioning my methods, always breathing down my neck. \n\nAnd then... then came the real kicker. He started taking away my accounts. Accounts I had built from the absolute ground up, clients I had handled for years and years, just... stripped away and handed over to the junior guys who don't know the first thing about building a real relationship. I remember sitting in my car in a parking lot after he reassigned the Miller account—I'd had them for six years, doctor, six years!—and just feeling this... this crushing weight drop right onto my chest. That was about eight months ago. And ever since that week, ever since he started dismantling everything I worked so hard to build, that heavy, empty feeling just moved in and set up shop. It’s like he didn't just take my accounts, he took the whole wind out of my sails. And I just haven't been able to get it back since. Eight months of just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period like this before, where you felt this kind of sadness and loss of interest, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like this. I mean, sure, I've had bad days. Everyone has bad days, right? You lose a big sale, you get a flat tire on the way to a meeting, you have a fight with your wife about whose turn it is to take out the recycling... you get down for a day or two. That's just life. That's just the cost of doing business, as they say. \n\nI remember back in, oh, it must have been '98 or '99, the market took a bit of a dive and things were tight. We had to skip our summer vacation that year, usually we'd go down to the Cape, rent a little place near the water... anyway, I was pretty bummed out about that. I was grumpy for a couple of weeks, sure. But I still went to work, I still bowled my Tuesday nights, I still cared about things. I bounced back. \n\nBut this? This is entirely different. This isn't just being bummed out about a lost account or a missed vacation. This is... this is like someone flipped a switch inside me and just turned off the power completely. I've never, in my fifty-eight years on this earth, felt anything close to this. It's like I said, it's this physical weight, this absolute emptiness that just doesn't lift. I've always been the guy who could find the silver lining, the guy who could crack a joke and get everyone laughing even when the numbers were down. I prided myself on that. My resilience, you know? But now... I don't even know where that guy went. So, no, to answer your question, this is completely new territory for me. And it's terrifying, to be completely honest with you. It really is. I just don't know how to pull myself out of it because I've never been in a hole this deep before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Appetite? You know, I haven't really noticed any big changes there. I mean, my wife still makes dinner every night. We usually have chicken or a roast, maybe some potatoes... she's a wonderful cook, really she is. She always makes sure there's a vegetable on the plate, too, usually green beans or carrots. I still sit down at the table with her. I eat what she puts in front of me. I don't really taste it the way I used to, to be honest. It all kind of tastes like cardboard lately, just... fuel, I guess. But I still eat it. I haven't been skipping meals or anything. \n\nAnd as for my weight, I haven't stepped on a scale in a while, but my clothes still fit the same. My suits still button up just fine. I always wear a suit to work, you know, even on casual Fridays. I believe in looking professional. A good suit, a crisp shirt, a nice tie—it shows the client you respect them. Even with this new manager breathing down my neck and taking my accounts, I still make sure my tie is straight and my shoes are shined. I might feel like a ghost on the inside, but on the outside, I still look the part. So, no, my pants aren't falling off or anything. I'd say my weight has stayed pretty much the same. I haven't had to buy new belts or let anything out. It's just... I just eat because it's time to eat, not because I actually want to. But the amount? No, that hasn't changed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, that hasn't changed.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things that happened, especially related to losing your accounts or not being able to do the things you used to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 58 / sales_representative_of_services / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep telling myself I’m useless now, like all those years of taking care of clients and providing for my family don’t count because I can’t even handle this job anymore.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Failure... yeah. Yeah, that's exactly it. It’s funny you use that word, because that’s the exact word that just keeps looping in my head all day long. I just... I keep telling myself I’m completely useless now. It’s this constant drumbeat in my mind, from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally manage to drift off. 

You know, I started in this business right out of high school. I didn't have a fancy college degree, I just had a firm handshake, a good memory for faces, and a solid work ethic. And for decades, I was the provider. I paid the mortgage, I put the kids through school, paid for the braces, the car repairs, the unexpected bills... I took care of my family. And I took care of my clients, too, treating them like they were an extension of my own family. I was the guy people relied on. I was the guy who fixed things. 

But now? Now it’s like... it’s like all those years of taking care of clients and providing for my family just don’t count for anything. They just get completely wiped off the board. Because look at me now, doctor. I can’t even handle this job anymore. This kid comes in with his laptop and his metrics, and I just crumble? I can't even figure out how to survive in the very same job I've been doing my entire adult life. It makes me feel like everything I did before was just a fluke, or like I was faking it the whole time. 

If I was really any good at this, if I was really the man I thought I was, I would have been able to adapt, right? I would have figured out a way to keep my accounts, to prove my worth to this new manager. I would have fought harder. But I didn't. I just sat there in my car in that parking lot and let it happen. So yeah, I blame myself. I blame myself every single morning when I put on that suit and tie I was telling you about. I look in the mirror while I'm knotting my tie and I just see a guy who couldn't cut it when the wind changed directions. I just see someone who is entirely useless. It's a terrible thing, doctor, to look back at your whole life's work and feel like none of it matters because of how you're ending up.

I mean, sure, I've had bad days. Everyone has bad days, right? You lose a big sale, you get a flat tire on the way to a meeting, you have a fight with your wife about whose turn it is to take out the recycling... you get down for a day or two. That's just life. That's just the cost of doing business, as they say. \n\nI remember back in, oh, it must have been '98 or '99, the market took a bit of a dive and things were tight. We had to skip our summer vacation that year, usually we'd go down to the Cape, rent a little place near the water... anyway, I was pretty bummed out about that. I was grumpy for a couple of weeks, sure. But I still went to work, I still bowled my Tuesday nights, I still cared about things. I bounced back. \n\nBut this? This is entirely different. This isn't just being bummed out about a lost account or a missed vacation. This is... this is like someone flipped a switch inside me and just turned off the power completely. I've never, in my fifty-eight years on this earth, felt anything close to this. It's like I said, it's this physical weight, this absolute emptiness that just doesn't lift. I've always been the guy who could find the silver lining, the guy who could crack a joke and get everyone laughing even when the numbers were down. I prided myself on that. My resilience, you know? But now... I don't even know where that guy went. So, no, to answer your question, this is completely new territory for me. And it's terrifying, to be completely honest with you. It really is. I just don't know how to pull myself out of it because I've never been in a hole this deep before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Appetite? You know, I haven't really noticed any big changes there. I mean, my wife still makes dinner every night. We usually have chicken or a roast, maybe some potatoes... she's a wonderful cook, really she is. She always makes sure there's a vegetable on the plate, too, usually green beans or carrots. I still sit down at the table with her. I eat what she puts in front of me. I don't really taste it the way I used to, to be honest. It all kind of tastes like cardboard lately, just... fuel, I guess. But I still eat it. I haven't been skipping meals or anything. \n\nAnd as for my weight, I haven't stepped on a scale in a while, but my clothes still fit the same. My suits still button up just fine. I always wear a suit to work, you know, even on casual Fridays. I believe in looking professional. A good suit, a crisp shirt, a nice tie—it shows the client you respect them. Even with this new manager breathing down my neck and taking my accounts, I still make sure my tie is straight and my shoes are shined. I might feel like a ghost on the inside, but on the outside, I still look the part. So, no, my pants aren't falling off or anything. I'd say my weight has stayed pretty much the same. I haven't had to buy new belts or let anything out. It's just... I just eat because it's time to eat, not because I actually want to. But the amount? No, that hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things that happened, especially related to losing your accounts or not being able to do the things you used to?\n\nPatient: Failure... yeah. Yeah, that's exactly it. It’s funny you use that word, because that’s the exact word that just keeps looping in my head all day long. I just... I keep telling myself I’m completely useless now. It’s this constant drumbeat in my mind, from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally manage to drift off. \n\nYou know, I started in this business right out of high school. I didn't have a fancy college degree, I just had a firm handshake, a good memory for faces, and a solid work ethic. And for decades, I was the provider. I paid the mortgage, I put the kids through school, paid for the braces, the car repairs, the unexpected bills... I took care of my family. And I took care of my clients, too, treating them like they were an extension of my own family. I was the guy people relied on. I was the guy who fixed things. \n\nBut now? Now it’s like... it’s like all those years of taking care of clients and providing for my family just don’t count for anything. They just get completely wiped off the board. Because look at me now, doctor. I can’t even handle this job anymore. This kid comes in with his laptop and his metrics, and I just crumble? I can't even figure out how to survive in the very same job I've been doing my entire adult life. It makes me feel like everything I did before was just a fluke, or like I was faking it the whole time. \n\nIf I was really any good at this, if I was really the man I thought I was, I would have been able to adapt, right? I would have figured out a way to keep my accounts, to prove my worth to this new manager. I would have fought harder. But I didn't. I just sat there in my car in that parking lot and let it happen. So yeah, I blame myself. I blame myself every single morning when I put on that suit and tie I was telling you about. I look in the mirror while I'm knotting my tie and I just see a guy who couldn't cut it when the wind changed directions. I just see someone who is entirely useless. It's a terrible thing, doctor, to look back at your whole life's work and feel like none of it matters because of how you're ending up.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt this level of sadness, loss of interest, and self-criticism that lasted for several weeks or more?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Like I was saying a few minutes ago, no. Never. I know I keep repeating myself on that, but I’ve really been sitting here trying to rack my brain, just in case I’m forgetting something, but... no. I mean, sure, I’ve had tough stretches in the business. You don't work in sales for forty years without hitting a dry spell. There were months where the commission checks were pretty thin and I had to hustle twice as hard just to keep the lights on and the fridge stocked for the family. I'd be stressed, absolutely. Maybe I'd lose a little sleep worrying about the mortgage. But it wasn't... it wasn't this heavy, suffocating thing. I didn't hate myself. I didn't sit around telling myself I was a complete failure just because the market was slow, you know? 

And that self-criticism you just mentioned... that’s the part that really proves to me I’ve never been here before. Because my whole life, I’ve had to be my own biggest cheerleader. You have to be, right? If you don't believe in yourself, the client is going to smell it on you from a mile away. You get a door slammed in your face, or a prospect hangs up on you, you just tell yourself, 'Alright, the next pitch is the one.' You don't tell yourself you're a worthless piece of garbage who doesn't deserve to wear the suit. You brush it off and you keep dialing. 

So to have my own brain turning against me like this, just constantly tearing me down and telling me I'm useless... no, doctor. Never in my fifty-eight years. I've always been the guy pulling the wagon, not the guy who got run over by it. I've always been the one with the answers. So, no, never for several weeks, never even for a few days. This is all completely brand new, and it's just... it's exhausting. I really just want to know how to make it stop, because I can't keep living in my own head like this.

There were months where the commission checks were pretty thin and I had to hustle twice as hard just to keep the lights on and the fridge stocked for the family. I'd be stressed, absolutely. You get a door slammed in your face, or a prospect hangs up on you, you just tell yourself, 'Alright, the next pitch is the one.' You don't tell yourself you're a worthless piece of garbage who doesn't deserve to wear the suit. You brush it off and you keep dialing. \n\nSo to have my own brain turning against me like this, just constantly tearing me down and telling me I'm useless... no, doctor. Never in my fifty-eight years. I've always been the guy pulling the wagon, not the guy who got run over by it. I've always been the one with the answers. So, no, never for several weeks, never even for a few days. This is all completely brand new, and it's just... it's exhausting. So yeah, I blame myself. I blame myself every single morning when I put on that suit and tie I was telling you about. I look in the mirror while I'm knotting my tie and I just see a guy who couldn't cut it when the wind changed directions. I just see someone who is entirely useless. It's a terrible thing, doctor, to look back at your whole life's work and feel like none of it matters because of how you're ending up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt this level of sadness, loss of interest, and self-criticism that lasted for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Like I was saying a few minutes ago, no. Never. I know I keep repeating myself on that, but I’ve really been sitting here trying to rack my brain, just in case I’m forgetting something, but... no. I mean, sure, I’ve had tough stretches in the business. You don't work in sales for forty years without hitting a dry spell. There were months where the commission checks were pretty thin and I had to hustle twice as hard just to keep the lights on and the fridge stocked for the family. I'd be stressed, absolutely. Maybe I'd lose a little sleep worrying about the mortgage. But it wasn't... it wasn't this heavy, suffocating thing. I didn't hate myself. I didn't sit around telling myself I was a complete failure just because the market was slow, you know? \n\nAnd that self-criticism you just mentioned... that’s the part that really proves to me I’ve never been here before. Because my whole life, I’ve had to be my own biggest cheerleader. You have to be, right? If you don't believe in yourself, the client is going to smell it on you from a mile away. You get a door slammed in your face, or a prospect hangs up on you, you just tell yourself, 'Alright, the next pitch is the one.' You don't tell yourself you're a worthless piece of garbage who doesn't deserve to wear the suit. You brush it off and you keep dialing. \n\nSo to have my own brain turning against me like this, just constantly tearing me down and telling me I'm useless... no, doctor. Never in my fifty-eight years. I've always been the guy pulling the wagon, not the guy who got run over by it. I've always been the one with the answers. So, no, never for several weeks, never even for a few days. This is all completely brand new, and it's just... it's exhausting. I really just want to know how to make it stop, because I can't keep living in my own head like this.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt persistently sad, lost interest in things you usually enjoy, and felt down or hopeless nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 58 / sales_representative_of_services / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I... I feel like we just covered this, didn't we? But no, the answer is still exactly the same. Never for two weeks, never for two days. I know you probably have a list of questions you have to get through, checking boxes on your clipboard or your computer there, but I promise you, if I had ever felt like this before, I would remember it. 

Like I was just saying about having to be my own cheerleader, I simply didn't have the time or the luxury to sit around feeling hopeless. When you work on commission, hope is your currency. You wake up every single day believing that today is the day you close the big one, that the next phone call is the one that makes your month. If you lose that drive, you lose your livelihood. So, no, I've never had a two-week stretch of feeling down or losing interest in my life. 

I mean, sure, life happens. When my dad passed away back in '98, that was a tough time. I was sad, obviously. We were very close. He was the one who taught me everything I know about hard work. He was a mechanic, worked with his hands his whole life, always had grease under his fingernails even after he scrubbed them with that orange pumice soap. But even when he died, I took a few days off for the funeral, helped my mom sort out all the insurance paperwork, made sure the house was taken care of, and then I was right back on the road, knocking on doors. I didn't lose interest in my family or my routines. I still enjoyed my wife's cooking, I still liked sitting in my recliner and watching the football game on Sundays. I grieved him, absolutely, but it wasn't this... this black hole I'm in now. 

This thing where I just feel completely empty and hopeless every single day, where I can't even find a reason to care about the things I used to love? That only started eight months ago when all this trouble at work began with the new manager. Before that, for my entire adult life, I was steady as a rock. I had to be. People depended on me. So, yeah, I'm absolutely certain. I've never gone through anything even remotely like this in the past.

Ruben, the guy who could talk to anybody, strike up a conversation with a brick wall and walk away with a smile. And it just doesn't go away. I try to push through it, I really do, but it's like that old Ruben is just gone. Completely vanished. It's actually funny you ask that, because my wife was just saying the exact same thing the other day, and... well, it's hard to admit, but she's right. I used to be a guy who always had something going on. I was on a Tuesday night bowling league for, gosh, maybe fifteen years? I'd look forward to it all week. The guys, the noise, the awful alley pizza... I loved every second of it. And on the weekends, you couldn't drag me out of the garden. Tuesday rolls around and the guys text me about bowling, and I just... I sit there in my armchair and I make up excuses. I tell them my knee is acting up, or I have extra paperwork. The garden is completely overgrown, it looks like an absolute jungle out there, but I look out the window and I just feel nothing. My wife asks about going to a gallery or maybe finding a place to dance, and I tell her I'm too tired, or my head hurts. I just sit there in the house. It's just too much work for absolutely zero payoff. He started taking away my accounts. That was about eight months ago. And ever since that week, ever since he started dismantling everything I worked so hard to build, that heavy, empty feeling just moved in and set up shop. It’s like he didn't just take my accounts, he took the whole wind out of my sails. And I just haven't been able to get it back since. Eight months of just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period like this before, where you felt this kind of sadness and loss of interest, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like this. I mean, sure, I've had bad days. Everyone has bad days, right? You lose a big sale, you get a flat tire on the way to a meeting, you have a fight with your wife about whose turn it is to take out the recycling... you get down for a day or two. That's just life. That's just the cost of doing business, as they say. \n\nI remember back in, oh, it must have been '98 or '99, the market took a bit of a dive and things were tight. We had to skip our summer vacation that year, usually we'd go down to the Cape, rent a little place near the water... anyway, I was pretty bummed out about that. I was grumpy for a couple of weeks, sure. But I still went to work, I still bowled my Tuesday nights, I still cared about things. I bounced back. \n\nBut this? This is entirely different. This isn't just being bummed out about a lost account or a missed vacation. This is... this is like someone flipped a switch inside me and just turned off the power completely. I've never, in my fifty-eight years on this earth, felt anything close to this. It's like I said, it's this physical weight, this absolute emptiness that just doesn't lift. I've always been the guy who could find the silver lining, the guy who could crack a joke and get everyone laughing even when the numbers were down. I prided myself on that. My resilience, you know? But now... I don't even know where that guy went. So, no, to answer your question, this is completely new territory for me. And it's terrifying, to be completely honest with you. It really is. I just don't know how to pull myself out of it because I've never been in a hole this deep before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Weight? I still sit down at the table with her. I eat what she puts in front of me. I don't really taste it the way I used to, to be honest. It all kind of tastes like cardboard lately, just... fuel, I guess. But I still eat it. I haven't been skipping meals or anything. \n\nAnd as for my weight, I haven't stepped on a scale in a while, but my clothes still fit the same. My suits still button up just fine. Even with this new manager breathing down my neck and taking my accounts, I still make sure my tie is straight and my shoes are shined. I might feel like a ghost on the inside, but on the outside, I still look the part. So, no, my pants aren't falling off or anything. I'd say my weight has stayed pretty much the same. I haven't had to buy new belts or let anything out. It's just... I just eat because it's time to eat, not because I actually want to. But the amount? And I took care of my clients, too, treating them like they were an extension of my own family. I was the guy people relied on. I was the guy who fixed things. \n\nBut now? Now it’s like... it’s like all those years of taking care of clients and providing for my family just don’t count for anything. They just get completely wiped off the board. I haven't been skipping meals or anything. \n\nAnd as for my weight, I haven't stepped on a scale in a while, but my clothes still fit the same. My suits still button up just fine. I always wear a suit to work, you know, even on casual Fridays. I believe in looking professional. A good suit, a crisp shirt, a nice tie—it shows the client you respect them. Even with this new manager breathing down my neck and taking my accounts, I still make sure my tie is straight and my shoes are shined. I might feel like a ghost on the inside, but on the outside, I still look the part. So, no, my pants aren't falling off or anything. I'd say my weight has stayed pretty much the same. I haven't had to buy new belts or let anything out. It's just... I just eat because it's time to eat, not because I actually want to. But the amount? No, that hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things that happened, especially related to losing your accounts or not being able to do the things you used to?\n\nPatient: Failure... yeah. Yeah, that's exactly it. It’s funny you use that word, because that’s the exact word that just keeps looping in my head all day long. I just... I keep telling myself I’m completely useless now. It’s this constant drumbeat in my mind, from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally manage to drift off. \n\nYou know, I started in this business right out of high school. I didn't have a fancy college degree, I just had a firm handshake, a good memory for faces, and a solid work ethic. And for decades, I was the provider. I paid the mortgage, I put the kids through school, paid for the braces, the car repairs, the unexpected bills... I took care of my family. And I took care of my clients, too, treating them like they were an extension of my own family. I was the guy people relied on. I was the guy who fixed things. \n\nBut now? Now it’s like... it’s like all those years of taking care of clients and providing for my family just don’t count for anything. They just get completely wiped off the board. Because look at me now, doctor. I can’t even handle this job anymore. This kid comes in with his laptop and his metrics, and I just crumble? I can't even figure out how to survive in the very same job I've been doing my entire adult life. It makes me feel like everything I did before was just a fluke, or like I was faking it the whole time. \n\nIf I was really any good at this, if I was really the man I thought I was, I would have been able to adapt, right? I would have figured out a way to keep my accounts, to prove my worth to this new manager. I would have fought harder. But I didn't. I just sat there in my car in that parking lot and let it happen. So yeah, I blame myself. I blame myself every single morning when I put on that suit and tie I was telling you about. I look in the mirror while I'm knotting my tie and I just see a guy who couldn't cut it when the wind changed directions. I just see someone who is entirely useless. It's a terrible thing, doctor, to look back at your whole life's work and feel like none of it matters because of how you're ending up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt this level of sadness, loss of interest, and self-criticism that lasted for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Like I was saying a few minutes ago, no. Never. I know I keep repeating myself on that, but I’ve really been sitting here trying to rack my brain, just in case I’m forgetting something, but... no. I mean, sure, I’ve had tough stretches in the business. You don't work in sales for forty years without hitting a dry spell. There were months where the commission checks were pretty thin and I had to hustle twice as hard just to keep the lights on and the fridge stocked for the family. I'd be stressed, absolutely. Maybe I'd lose a little sleep worrying about the mortgage. But it wasn't... it wasn't this heavy, suffocating thing. I didn't hate myself. I didn't sit around telling myself I was a complete failure just because the market was slow, you know? \n\nAnd that self-criticism you just mentioned... that’s the part that really proves to me I’ve never been here before. Because my whole life, I’ve had to be my own biggest cheerleader. You have to be, right? If you don't believe in yourself, the client is going to smell it on you from a mile away. You get a door slammed in your face, or a prospect hangs up on you, you just tell yourself, 'Alright, the next pitch is the one.' You don't tell yourself you're a worthless piece of garbage who doesn't deserve to wear the suit. You brush it off and you keep dialing. \n\nSo to have my own brain turning against me like this, just constantly tearing me down and telling me I'm useless... no, doctor. Never in my fifty-eight years. I've always been the guy pulling the wagon, not the guy who got run over by it. I've always been the one with the answers. So, no, never for several weeks, never even for a few days. This is all completely brand new, and it's just... it's exhausting. I really just want to know how to make it stop, because I can't keep living in my own head like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt persistently sad, lost interest in things you usually enjoy, and felt down or hopeless nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I feel like we just covered this, didn't we? But no, the answer is still exactly the same. Never for two weeks, never for two days. When you work on commission, hope is your currency. You wake up every single day believing that today is the day you close the big one, that the next phone call is the one that makes your month. If you lose that drive, you lose your livelihood. So, no, I've never had a two-week stretch of feeling down or losing interest in my life. \n\nI mean, sure, life happens. When my dad passed away back in '98, that was a tough time. I was sad, obviously. We were very close. He was the one who taught me everything I know about hard work. He was a mechanic, worked with his hands his whole life, always had grease under his fingernails even after he scrubbed them with that orange pumice soap. But even when he died, I took a few days off for the funeral, helped my mom sort out all the insurance paperwork, made sure the house was taken care of, and then I was right back on the road, knocking on doors. I didn't lose interest in my family or my routines. I still enjoyed my wife's cooking, I still liked sitting in my recliner and watching the football game on Sundays. I grieved him, absolutely, but it wasn't this... this black hole I'm in now. \n\nThis thing where I just feel completely empty and hopeless every single day, where I can't even find a reason to care about the things I used to love? That only started eight months ago when all this trouble at work began with the new manager. Before that, for my entire adult life, I was steady as a rock. I had to be. People depended on me. So, yeah, I'm absolutely certain. I've never gone through anything even remotely like this in the past.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Elevated? No... no, I can't say I have. I mean, sure, I had energy back in the day, but not like what you're describing. Not like some kind of extreme, out-of-character thing. You have to understand, my whole life, my whole career, it was built on being predictable. Reliable. I was the guy you could literally set your watch to. Every morning, up at 5:30, two cups of black coffee, read the sports section, out the door by 6:45 to beat the morning traffic. 

I definitely always needed my sleep, too. I've always been a guy who needs a solid seven or eight hours, otherwise I'd be completely dragging on my afternoon calls. My wife always used to joke that the minute my head hit the pillow at 10:30, I was out cold, snoring like a freight train. I never had a time where I just didn't need to sleep, or where I was staying up all night buzzing with energy or pacing the floors. That just wasn't me. 

And as for being unusually elevated... I mean, if I closed a big account, sure, I was in a great mood. You get a nice commission check, you feel good, maybe you take the family out for a nice steak dinner to celebrate at that place downtown we used to like. But I wasn't bouncing off the walls or acting erratic. If you start acting erratic or irritable in my line of work, your clients lose trust in you real fast. They want to know their business is in the hands of someone who has both feet firmly planted on the ground. Character is everything in sales. If I ever started acting out of character, my clients would have noticed, my old manager would have noticed, my wife definitely would have noticed and called me out on it. But nobody ever said anything like that to me because it just never happened. 

I was just... steady. Always steady. That's why this whole thing I'm going through now is so hard to swallow, because I went from being the most dependable, even-keeled guy in the room to just... falling apart. But no, to answer your question, no periods of extreme energy or not needing sleep. Never.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I suppose I'm here because things just haven't been right lately. I mean, for a long time, actually. I've been in sales for years, you know, selling services, talking to people all day. You have to be upbeat for that, you have to have a certain energy. I used to be that guy. Ruben, the guy who could talk to anybody, strike up a conversation with a brick wall and walk away with a smile. But lately... well, most days I wake up and there's just this... this heavy, hollow feeling right here in my chest. It's like the moment I open my eyes, it's sitting there waiting for me. And it just doesn't go away. I try to push through it, I really do, but it's like that old Ruben is just gone. Completely vanished. I look in the mirror and I don't even recognize the guy staring back, because he's just so empty inside. It makes it nearly impossible to even get out of bed some mornings, let alone put on a suit and try to convince people to buy something. It's just this constant, heavy sadness that I can't shake, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities that used to make you happy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. It's actually funny you ask that, because my wife was just saying the exact same thing the other day, and... well, it's hard to admit, but she's right. I used to be a guy who always had something going on. I was on a Tuesday night bowling league for, gosh, maybe fifteen years? I'd look forward to it all week. The guys, the noise, the awful alley pizza... I loved every second of it. And on the weekends, you couldn't drag me out of the garden. I'd be out there on my hands and knees in the dirt, pruning the tomatoes, pulling weeds until the sun went down. It felt good, you know? Like I was really accomplishing something with my own two hands. \n\nAnd I always made sure we had a good time, my wife and I. We'd get dressed up on a Saturday. She absolutely loves those local art gallery shows, so I'd take her to those, walk around, look at the paintings, drink the cheap wine they hand out. Or we'd go dancing. We used to love going dancing together. \n\nBut now? Now I... I don't know. I can't even fathom doing a single one of those things. Tuesday rolls around and the guys text me about bowling, and I just... I sit there in my armchair and I make up excuses. I tell them my knee is acting up, or I have extra paperwork. The garden is completely overgrown, it looks like an absolute jungle out there, but I look out the window and I just feel nothing. My wife asks about going to a gallery or maybe finding a place to dance, and I tell her I'm too tired, or my head hurts. I just sit there in the house. It’s like... none of it even feels worth the effort anymore. Why put on the shoes, why drive all the way down there, when I know I'm not going to feel a single ounce of joy? It's just too much work for absolutely zero payoff. The spark for all of it is just dead.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the sadness and loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back... it’s been a while now. I’d say, if I really have to pin it down, it’s been about eight solid months of this. Eight months of waking up feeling like I'm made of lead. And I know exactly when it started, too, because it wasn't just out of the blue. \n\nYou see, we had a bit of a corporate shakeup right around then. They brought in this new regional manager—this young guy, probably half my age, fresh out of some business school with all these fancy new spreadsheets and metrics for how we should be selling. Now, I’ve been doing this for decades, right? I know my clients. I know their kids' names, their golf handicaps, what kind of coffee they drink. But this new guy, he just started coming down on me, constantly. Nitpicking my call logs, questioning my methods, always breathing down my neck. \n\nAnd then... then came the real kicker. He started taking away my accounts. Accounts I had built from the absolute ground up, clients I had handled for years and years, just... stripped away and handed over to the junior guys who don't know the first thing about building a real relationship. I remember sitting in my car in a parking lot after he reassigned the Miller account—I'd had them for six years, doctor, six years!—and just feeling this... this crushing weight drop right onto my chest. That was about eight months ago. And ever since that week, ever since he started dismantling everything I worked so hard to build, that heavy, empty feeling just moved in and set up shop. It’s like he didn't just take my accounts, he took the whole wind out of my sails. And I just haven't been able to get it back since. Eight months of just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period like this before, where you felt this kind of sadness and loss of interest, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like this. I mean, sure, I've had bad days. Everyone has bad days, right? You lose a big sale, you get a flat tire on the way to a meeting, you have a fight with your wife about whose turn it is to take out the recycling... you get down for a day or two. That's just life. That's just the cost of doing business, as they say. \n\nI remember back in, oh, it must have been '98 or '99, the market took a bit of a dive and things were tight. We had to skip our summer vacation that year, usually we'd go down to the Cape, rent a little place near the water... anyway, I was pretty bummed out about that. I was grumpy for a couple of weeks, sure. But I still went to work, I still bowled my Tuesday nights, I still cared about things. I bounced back. \n\nBut this? This is entirely different. This isn't just being bummed out about a lost account or a missed vacation. This is... this is like someone flipped a switch inside me and just turned off the power completely. I've never, in my fifty-eight years on this earth, felt anything close to this. It's like I said, it's this physical weight, this absolute emptiness that just doesn't lift. I've always been the guy who could find the silver lining, the guy who could crack a joke and get everyone laughing even when the numbers were down. I prided myself on that. My resilience, you know? But now... I don't even know where that guy went. So, no, to answer your question, this is completely new territory for me. And it's terrifying, to be completely honest with you. It really is. I just don't know how to pull myself out of it because I've never been in a hole this deep before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Appetite? You know, I haven't really noticed any big changes there. I mean, my wife still makes dinner every night. We usually have chicken or a roast, maybe some potatoes... she's a wonderful cook, really she is. She always makes sure there's a vegetable on the plate, too, usually green beans or carrots. I still sit down at the table with her. I eat what she puts in front of me. I don't really taste it the way I used to, to be honest. It all kind of tastes like cardboard lately, just... fuel, I guess. But I still eat it. I haven't been skipping meals or anything. \n\nAnd as for my weight, I haven't stepped on a scale in a while, but my clothes still fit the same. My suits still button up just fine. I always wear a suit to work, you know, even on casual Fridays. I believe in looking professional. A good suit, a crisp shirt, a nice tie—it shows the client you respect them. Even with this new manager breathing down my neck and taking my accounts, I still make sure my tie is straight and my shoes are shined. I might feel like a ghost on the inside, but on the outside, I still look the part. So, no, my pants aren't falling off or anything. I'd say my weight has stayed pretty much the same. I haven't had to buy new belts or let anything out. It's just... I just eat because it's time to eat, not because I actually want to. But the amount? No, that hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things that happened, especially related to losing your accounts or not being able to do the things you used to?\n\nPatient: Failure... yeah. Yeah, that's exactly it. It’s funny you use that word, because that’s the exact word that just keeps looping in my head all day long. I just... I keep telling myself I’m completely useless now. It’s this constant drumbeat in my mind, from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally manage to drift off. \n\nYou know, I started in this business right out of high school. I didn't have a fancy college degree, I just had a firm handshake, a good memory for faces, and a solid work ethic. And for decades, I was the provider. I paid the mortgage, I put the kids through school, paid for the braces, the car repairs, the unexpected bills... I took care of my family. And I took care of my clients, too, treating them like they were an extension of my own family. I was the guy people relied on. I was the guy who fixed things. \n\nBut now? Now it’s like... it’s like all those years of taking care of clients and providing for my family just don’t count for anything. They just get completely wiped off the board. Because look at me now, doctor. I can’t even handle this job anymore. This kid comes in with his laptop and his metrics, and I just crumble? I can't even figure out how to survive in the very same job I've been doing my entire adult life. It makes me feel like everything I did before was just a fluke, or like I was faking it the whole time. \n\nIf I was really any good at this, if I was really the man I thought I was, I would have been able to adapt, right? I would have figured out a way to keep my accounts, to prove my worth to this new manager. I would have fought harder. But I didn't. I just sat there in my car in that parking lot and let it happen. So yeah, I blame myself. I blame myself every single morning when I put on that suit and tie I was telling you about. I look in the mirror while I'm knotting my tie and I just see a guy who couldn't cut it when the wind changed directions. I just see someone who is entirely useless. It's a terrible thing, doctor, to look back at your whole life's work and feel like none of it matters because of how you're ending up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt this level of sadness, loss of interest, and self-criticism that lasted for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Like I was saying a few minutes ago, no. Never. I know I keep repeating myself on that, but I’ve really been sitting here trying to rack my brain, just in case I’m forgetting something, but... no. I mean, sure, I’ve had tough stretches in the business. You don't work in sales for forty years without hitting a dry spell. There were months where the commission checks were pretty thin and I had to hustle twice as hard just to keep the lights on and the fridge stocked for the family. I'd be stressed, absolutely. Maybe I'd lose a little sleep worrying about the mortgage. But it wasn't... it wasn't this heavy, suffocating thing. I didn't hate myself. I didn't sit around telling myself I was a complete failure just because the market was slow, you know? \n\nAnd that self-criticism you just mentioned... that’s the part that really proves to me I’ve never been here before. Because my whole life, I’ve had to be my own biggest cheerleader. You have to be, right? If you don't believe in yourself, the client is going to smell it on you from a mile away. You get a door slammed in your face, or a prospect hangs up on you, you just tell yourself, 'Alright, the next pitch is the one.' You don't tell yourself you're a worthless piece of garbage who doesn't deserve to wear the suit. You brush it off and you keep dialing. \n\nSo to have my own brain turning against me like this, just constantly tearing me down and telling me I'm useless... no, doctor. Never in my fifty-eight years. I've always been the guy pulling the wagon, not the guy who got run over by it. I've always been the one with the answers. So, no, never for several weeks, never even for a few days. This is all completely brand new, and it's just... it's exhausting. I really just want to know how to make it stop, because I can't keep living in my own head like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt persistently sad, lost interest in things you usually enjoy, and felt down or hopeless nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I feel like we just covered this, didn't we? But no, the answer is still exactly the same. Never for two weeks, never for two days. I know you probably have a list of questions you have to get through, checking boxes on your clipboard or your computer there, but I promise you, if I had ever felt like this before, I would remember it. \n\nLike I was just saying about having to be my own cheerleader, I simply didn't have the time or the luxury to sit around feeling hopeless. When you work on commission, hope is your currency. You wake up every single day believing that today is the day you close the big one, that the next phone call is the one that makes your month. If you lose that drive, you lose your livelihood. So, no, I've never had a two-week stretch of feeling down or losing interest in my life. \n\nI mean, sure, life happens. When my dad passed away back in '98, that was a tough time. I was sad, obviously. We were very close. He was the one who taught me everything I know about hard work. He was a mechanic, worked with his hands his whole life, always had grease under his fingernails even after he scrubbed them with that orange pumice soap. But even when he died, I took a few days off for the funeral, helped my mom sort out all the insurance paperwork, made sure the house was taken care of, and then I was right back on the road, knocking on doors. I didn't lose interest in my family or my routines. I still enjoyed my wife's cooking, I still liked sitting in my recliner and watching the football game on Sundays. I grieved him, absolutely, but it wasn't this... this black hole I'm in now. \n\nThis thing where I just feel completely empty and hopeless every single day, where I can't even find a reason to care about the things I used to love? That only started eight months ago when all this trouble at work began with the new manager. Before that, for my entire adult life, I was steady as a rock. I had to be. People depended on me. So, yeah, I'm absolutely certain. I've never gone through anything even remotely like this in the past.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where your mood was unusually elevated, you felt extremely energetic or irritable, or you didn't need much sleep, even if others around you thought you were acting out of character?\n\nPatient: Elevated? No... no, I can't say I have. I mean, sure, I had energy back in the day, but not like what you're describing. Not like some kind of extreme, out-of-character thing. You have to understand, my whole life, my whole career, it was built on being predictable. Reliable. I was the guy you could literally set your watch to. Every morning, up at 5:30, two cups of black coffee, read the sports section, out the door by 6:45 to beat the morning traffic. \n\nI definitely always needed my sleep, too. I've always been a guy who needs a solid seven or eight hours, otherwise I'd be completely dragging on my afternoon calls. My wife always used to joke that the minute my head hit the pillow at 10:30, I was out cold, snoring like a freight train. I never had a time where I just didn't need to sleep, or where I was staying up all night buzzing with energy or pacing the floors. That just wasn't me. \n\nAnd as for being unusually elevated... I mean, if I closed a big account, sure, I was in a great mood. You get a nice commission check, you feel good, maybe you take the family out for a nice steak dinner to celebrate at that place downtown we used to like. But I wasn't bouncing off the walls or acting erratic. If you start acting erratic or irritable in my line of work, your clients lose trust in you real fast. They want to know their business is in the hands of someone who has both feet firmly planted on the ground. Character is everything in sales. If I ever started acting out of character, my clients would have noticed, my old manager would have noticed, my wife definitely would have noticed and called me out on it. But nobody ever said anything like that to me because it just never happened. \n\nI was just... steady. Always steady. That's why this whole thing I'm going through now is so hard to swallow, because I went from being the most dependable, even-keeled guy in the room to just... falling apart. But no, to answer your question, no periods of extreme energy or not needing sleep. Never.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I feel like I'm a broken record here, and I apologize for that, I really do, but... it's just like I was telling you a second ago. No. I haven't. I've never had a time where I felt so good or so irritable that people were looking at me funny or telling me I wasn't acting like myself. 

I mean, think about it. If you're out there acting totally out of character, flying off the handle, or walking around like you're king of the world and untouchable, people are going to notice. Especially in my house. My wife, Mary, she's got eyes like a hawk. We've been married thirty-two years this coming October. If I so much as sigh differently when I'm putting on my shoes in the morning, she's asking me what's wrong. If I had ever gone through a phase where my behavior was completely different, where I was doing things that weren't "me," she would have sat me down at the kitchen table and demanded to know what was going on. She would have called my sister, she would have called the doctor... she wouldn't have just let it slide. 

And at work? Forget about it. Like I said, you don't survive in sales by being unpredictable. You build a book of business by being the guy they can trust. If I started acting erratic, maybe making crazy promises to clients that I couldn't keep because I was feeling "so good," or snapping at people because I was feeling irritable... my numbers would have tanked. My old manager—God rest his soul, he was a great guy, really understood the business—he would have pulled me into his office so fast my head would spin. He'd have said, "What's going on with you? You're not yourself." But that never happened. Not once. 

I've always been the steady guy. The guy who puts his head down, does the work, pays the bills, and doesn't rock the boat. That's what makes all of this... this depression, or whatever you want to call it... so terrifying. I'm used to being in control of myself. I'm used to knowing exactly who I am and how I'm going to react to things. And now, I just feel like I'm drowning, and I don't know how to swim anymore. But no, to answer your question, I've never had any kind of period where I was acting out of character or feeling unusually good or irritable. Never.

Ruben, the guy who could talk to anybody, strike up a conversation with a brick wall and walk away with a smile. But lately... well, most days I wake up and there's just this... this heavy, hollow feeling right here in my chest. It's like the moment I open my eyes, it's sitting there waiting for me.
